3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work
We propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [13,14]; Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [15], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of worldwide priority.
[["Planned output", "What we expect to learn"], ["C1. Forecast-ready table", "Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits."], ["C2. Boosted-tree forecast", "Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest."], ["C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment", "Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history."]]
3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope
Use 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that all six signals help.
Boosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each correction [18]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7. Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [16]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows [17]. They provide a linear and a tree-ensemble reference for the same task.
Initial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages {100,200,300} × learning rates {0.03,0.1}, depth=2, minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max_features=1.0, depth {6,10,14} × minimum leaf {10,40}. Ridge alpha: {0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set, chosen by validation MAE [16,17,18]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.
3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement
Train January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the 2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1, PUMP_1, n4/n31 or AMR_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.
Recompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE_model/MAE_persistence (positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these comparisons do not prove statistical significance. ## 2026-10-18T23:43:02.211Z · source line 7701 · Assistant message · AgentMessage

Source: `original_sessions/rollout-2026-10-11T10-33-18-01a12b61-e72c-7da1-bb50-45bff9d85d03.jsonl#L7701`  
Event ID: `msg_02d303a6a82c3a1a016ad5597c9d7c81a2ace295f58f3ba3a4`  
Turn ID: `01a15165-335b-7392-9234-56d42984ac24`

### Message

```text
**Tu contribución propuesta es evaluar si Gradient Boosting puede mejorar el pronóstico de presión de n1 a 15 minutos y determinar cuánto ayudan las mediciones del sector a conseguirlo.**

La pregunta central sería:

> **¿Podemos pronosticar mejor la presión futura de n1 incorporando información del tanque, la bomba, los nodos vecinos y la demanda local, en comparación con utilizar solamente la historia de n1?**

La sección plantea tres aportes:

1. **Preparar los datos para un pronóstico reproducible.**  
   Para cada instante, construirías una fila con información disponible hasta ese momento y la asociarías con la presión registrada 15 minutos después. Usarías la lectura actual y los últimos 55 minutos de las seis señales: **n1, n4, n31, T1, PUMP_1 y AMR_n1**, además del calendario. Esta preparación permite evaluar el pronóstico sin introducir información futura.

2. **Evaluar un método alternativo: Gradient Boosting.**  
   Este método combina árboles pequeños de manera secuencial: cada árbol intenta corregir errores que todavía comete el conjunto. Lo compararías con persistencia —suponer que la presión seguirá igual—, regresión Ridge y Random Forest. **Su ventaja debe demostrarse mediante menores errores; todavía no sabemos si ganará.**

3. **Identificar qué información adicional aporta valor.**  
   Compararías el modelo completo con otro que utilice únicamente la historia de n1 y el calendario. Después retirarías grupos de variables para comprobar, por ejemplo, si conocer el nivel del tanque o el caudal de la bomba mejora realmente el pronóstico. Una relación hidráulica razonable no garantiza utilidad predictiva.

**¿Dónde está la novedad?** En la revisión no identificamos este experimento exacto: **Gradient Boosting para n1 a +15 minutos, con esas seis señales del SCADA oficial y una evaluación explícita del aporte de las variables auxiliares**. Es una contribución experimental y de análisis; Gradient Boosting ya existe y la búsqueda realizada no permite asegurar que esta combinación nunca haya sido estudiada.

La evaluarías respetando el orden temporal: desarrollar y seleccionar modelos con 2018 y realizar la evaluación posterior con 2019, usando MAE y RMSE. Incluso si alguna variable no ayuda, ese resultado permitiría justificar un modelo más sencillo. **Actualmente esto es una propuesta: los modelos todavía no se han entrenado.**

```

### aggregated_output

````text
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final Response Citations

- **Create/edit:** for created or edited PDFs, cite each final deliverable exactly once with `purpose="output"`, in its own paragraph. Briefly describe the result without repeating its filename, path, or link. Never put output directives inside prose or list items.
- **Q&A and summaries:** Every file whose contents you summarize or use to support an answer MUST receive a `:codex-file-citation{...}` with `purpose="source"` in the final response. Use the source citation in place of the filename, wherever the filename would appear. Do not write the filename separately and append its citation at the end. Files merely inspected but not used in the answer need no citation. Do not emit output citations for unchanged files or read-only answers.

- Emit `:codex-file-citation{...}` directly, without backticks, code fences, or a Markdown-link wrapper. Use verified targets only. No need to cite or reference intermediate work.

For file citations, set `path` to the verified absolute file path on the task host. Omit `mode`.

 Mini-Project Milestone 2
Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
1/5
1. Description of Dataset and Learning Task
1.1 Source, records and suitability
BattLeDIM is an open, simulated drinking-water benchmark representing L-Town, inspired by Cyprus. It was created to compare
leak detection/localization methods. Its continuous time series also allow a supervised pressure-forecasting task. Zenodo supplies
supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) records, leak labels, sensor configuration and EPANET hydraulic models [1,2].
These are simulated observations, not field measurements.
The 2018-2019 release has five-minute spacing: 105,120 timestamps per year, 210,240 total, and 119 numerical measurement
channels per timestamp. The nominal network contains 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and
three pressure-reducing valves (PRVs). Nominal and generator parameters differ [1].
Quantity
Channels / units
Meaning
Pressure
33; m water head
Pressure at measured junctions.
Pipe/pump flow
3; m3/h
PUMP_1 and p227/p235 flow.
Tank level
1; m
Water level in T1.
AMR demand
82; L/h
Automated meter reading (AMR): consumption assigned to junctions, not pipe
inflow.
Our checks of all eight annual SCADA tables found matching, unique timestamps and no missing values. The benchmark assumes
error-free sensors and no transmission delay/loss. Values are rounded to two decimals; pressure readings average the preceding five
minutes [2], Sensors and Telemetry. Nearby time records are related, so the later evaluation keeps time order. Leak labels are not
model inputs.
1.2 Learning task and hydraulic feature selection
The task is single-output supervised regression: y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min), three samples ahead, in metres. The label is the recorded
five-minute average ending at u+15, including the simulated operating/leak conditions. Each input row is paired with n1 three
timestamps later; there are no target classes. The intended use is short-term monitoring at a remote measured junction. Every
input must be available at forecast time u.
Figure 1. Functional supply boundary derived from the released model [1]. Nominal PRV settings are 40/50/35 m; constant settings do not fix
remote-node pressures.
Area C is supplied through T1 and has 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. Our nominal-model checks give n1
the longest T1-to-sensor pipe path: 891 m, versus 574/308 m for n4/n31; this distance is not a signal delay. We keep n1/n4/n31
pressure, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand: 119 channels →87 local/boundary candidates →six signals. T1
represents storage, PUMP_1 measured inflow, n4/n31 the local pressure state, and AMR_n1 consumption at the target junction.
Unlike PRV-3’s single measured pressure, C provides several related measurements. Whether they help n1 forecasting is a question
to test.

2/5
1. Description of Dataset and Learning Task (continued)
1.3 Own exploratory analysis
Our exploratory data analysis (EDA) uses January-September 2018 only: 273 days and 78,624 timestamps. n1 ranges from 25.67
to 29.36 m. The generator opens PUMP_1 below T1=2.4 m and closes it above 3.9 m [1]. Figure 2 shows how pressure, tank level
and pump operation vary through the day; the mean curve does not describe every individual day.
Figure 2. Own EDA: T1/PUMP_1 at left; n1/n4/n31 at right. Thin lines: individual days; red: clock-time mean.
For 78,621 valid +15 pairs, excluding the final three origins, persistence predicts the current n1 value. Its mean absolute error
(MAE) is 0.0518 m (about 5.18 cm); root mean squared error (RMSE) is 0.0685 m and gives more weight to large errors. These are
our 2018 descriptive results, not final test scores. Correlation with future n1 is 0.9923 for current n1, 0.9230 for T1, -0.0367 for
PUMP_1 and -0.1574 for AMR_n1. Pressure changes slowly over 15 minutes; high correlation alone does not show that an extra
signal improves a forecast.
Figure 3. Own EDA: six origin inputs and the future label. The label is separated for analysis and never enters X.

2. Contributions of Previous Authors
We searched BattLeDIM/L-Town predictive models and followed citations/code. Related papers/repositories form 35 groups of
related works on L-Town or explicit modifications; external networks, reviews and tools were excluded. The five categories mean
leak detection/location, current-pressure estimation, future-pressure/head forecasting, anomaly/sensor-fault detection, and sensor
placement. This inventory is not exhaustive.
Figure 4. Own screening: percentages use 35 groups; gold identifies six selected subsets. Primary diagnostic purpose can include an intermediate
future-pressure prediction.
ChebNet/SAM-kNN/AGN include future prediction; LILA/GATRes estimate current pressure; EILF explains Area C’s water
balance. SAM-kNN, AGN and GATRes use newly generated L-Town data rather than the released SCADA.
Study / task / code
Data preparation and how the method works
Reported results and what they show
ChebNet [3] +5-min
pressure and leakage
diagnosis. Code: [4].
Data: Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and
three past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split.
Models: Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts
healthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current
pressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over
seven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were
compared. Code fits scaling before the split.
2019 diagnosis, Table 1: 19 accepted matches,
138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61%,
EUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300
m while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain;
these results do not measure forecast-pressure error.
SAM-kNN [5] +5-min
virtual pressure;
anomalies. Code: [6];
scenarios not shared.
Data: Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29
sensors). Code averages three past readings from other
sensors to predict the target’s next reading (+5 min);
paper describes averaging four. Model: Adaptive
nearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples
and selects the memory with the lowest recent prediction
error.
Five leak cases, Table 1: Each leak is detected,
with 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus
11,146-19,216 for ordinary online kNN. These are
alarm counts, not pressure errors. The scenarios are
newly simulated, not the released 2018-2019 series.
AGN [7] Multistep
future nodal heads.
Code: no repository
linked; data on request.
Data: One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min
samples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10
train/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use
analysis guides features. Model: Graph links share
information; recurrent units retain history; attention
weights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head +
elevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs.
Future heads, Table 1: 12/24/48/96-step outputs
reach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window,
regional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium),
32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole
windows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate
n1(+15) score is reported.
LILA [8] Current
healthy-pressure
reference; leaks. Code:
[9].
Data: Released SCADA; analyze pressure/flow
relationships to select reference sensors and healthy
periods. Model: Linear regression estimates current
healthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds
persistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic
calculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw
PUMP_1. It is not a future forecast.
Journal Table 1: 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited
leaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision
89.5%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations
outside 300 m count as false positives, not missed
events; recall=1 therefore does not mean perfect
localization.
GATRes [10] Current
sparse-to-full pressure.
Code: [11].
Data: 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20
train/validation/test split); compare pressure
distributions with time-series test data. Model: Hide
selected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph
attention weights neighboring information to reconstruct
the hidden pressures at the same time point.
One-week test, Table 10: Across ten training
runs, MAE=0.9501±0.0086 m versus 1.5928±0.0050
m for the mGCN graph baseline. The ± is the
standard deviation between runs. This measures
current-pressure reconstruction, not +15-min
forecasting.
EILF [12] Engineering
diagnosis, not future
learning. Code: on
request.
Data: 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump
inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector
outflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min
windows to reduce level-rounding effects. Method:
Separate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL),
detect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically.
Journal conclusion, p. 11: TPR=47.83%, five
false positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak
diagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The
tank-balance calculation is an existing engineering
contribution relevant to our feature selection.
TP/FP/FN count accepted matches, false alarms/wrong matches and missed leaks; TPR=recall; F1 combines precision/recall.
EUR is the competition monetary score [2]. Tasks/data differ, so these scores cannot rank n1 forecast accuracy. Graph filters,
adaptive memories and CUSUM extend class baselines; their source files show the mechanisms [4,6,9]. Training was not reproduced.

3. Your Anticipated Contributions
3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work
We propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level
alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [13,14];
Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [15], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this
exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including
operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of
worldwide priority.
Planned output
What we expect to learn
C1. Forecast-ready table
Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits.
C2. Boosted-tree forecast
Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest.
C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment
Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history.
3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope
Use 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven
weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check
units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit
Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that
all six signals help.
bpn1(u + 15) = FM(X(u)),
Fm(X) = Fm−1(X) + ηhm(X).
Boosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each
correction [18]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7.
Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [16]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows
[17]. They provide a linear and a tree-ensemble reference for the same task.
Initial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages {100,200,300} × learning rates {0.03,0.1}, depth=2,
minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max_features=1.0, depth
{6,10,14} × minimum leaf {10,40}. Ridge alpha: {0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set,
chosen by validation MAE [16,17,18]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.
3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement
Train January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the
2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that
cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based
assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1,
PUMP_1, n4/n31 or AMR_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.
Recompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE_model/MAE_persistence
(positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary
value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these
comparisons do not prove statistical significance. Deliver the preparation/model notebooks, settings, score table and prediction/error
plots. No learning model has been fitted. Delays/ages, balance proxies and block intervals remain outside this scope.

PDF citations support only plain file citations; do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators.\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 1 \\r\\n Mini-Project Milestone 2\\r\\nLearning Models for Pressure Forecasting\\r\\nDS675\\r\\nAndres Arango Martinez\\r\\n1/5\\r\\n1. Description of Dataset and Learning Task\\r\\n1.1 Source, records and suitability\\r\\nBattLeDIM is an open, simulated drinking-water benchmark representing L-Town, inspired by Cyprus. It was created to compare\\r\\nleak detection/localization methods. Its continuous time series also allow a supervised pressure-forecasting task. Zenodo supplies\\r\\nsupervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) records, leak labels, sensor configuration and EPANET hydraulic models [1,2].\\r\\nThese are simulated observations, not field measurements.\\r\\nThe 2018-2019 release has five-minute spacing: 105,120 timestamps per year, 210,240 total, and 119 numerical measurement\\r\\nchannels per timestamp. The nominal network contains 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and\\r\\nthree pressure-reducing valves (PRVs). Nominal and generator parameters differ [1].\\r\\nQuantity\\r\\nChannels / units\\r\\nMeaning\\r\\nPressure\\r\\n33; m water head\\r\\nPressure at measured junctions.\\r\\nPipe/pump flow\\r\\n3; m3/h\\r\\nPUMP_1 and p227/p235 flow.\\r\\nTank level\\r\\n1; m\\r\\nWater level in T1.\\r\\nAMR demand\\r\\n82; L/h\\r\\nAutomated meter reading (AMR): consumption assigned to junctions, not pipe\\r\\ninflow.\\r\\nOur checks of all eight annual SCADA tables found matching, unique timestamps and no missing values. The benchmark assumes\\r\\nerror-free sensors and no transmission delay/loss. Values are rounded to two decimals; pressure readings average the preceding five\\r\\nminutes [2], Sensors and Telemetry. Nearby time records are related, so the later evaluation keeps time order. Leak labels are not\\r\\nmodel inputs.\\r\\n1.2 Learning task and hydraulic feature selection\\r\\nThe task is single-output supervised regression: y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min), three samples ahead, in metres. The label is the recorded\\r\\nfive-minute average ending at u+15, including the simulated operating/leak conditions. Each input row is paired with n1 three\\r\\ntimestamps later; there are no target classes. The intended use is short-term monitoring at a remote measured junction. Every\\r\\ninput must be available at forecast time u.\\r\\nFigure 1. Functional supply boundary derived from the released model [1]. Nominal PRV settings are 40/50/35 m; constant settings do not fix\\r\\nremote-node pressures.\\r\\nArea C is supplied through T1 and has 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. Our nominal-model checks give n1\\r\\nthe longest T1-to-sensor pipe path: 891 m, versus 574/308 m for n4/n31; this distance is not a signal delay. We keep n1/n4/n31\\r\\npressure, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand: 119 channels →87 local/boundary candidates →six signals. T1\\r\\nrepresents storage, PUMP_1 measured inflow, n4/n31 the local pressure state, and AMR_n1 consumption at the target junction.\\r\\nUnlike PRV-3’s single measured pressure, C provides several related measurements. Whether they help n1 forecasting is a question\\r\\nto test.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 2 \\r\\n Mini-Project Milestone 2\\r\\nLearning Models for Pressure Forecasting\\r\\nDS675\\r\\nAndres Arango Martinez\\r\\n2/5\\r\\n1. Description of Dataset and Learning Task (continued)\\r\\n1.3 Own exploratory analysis\\r\\nOur exploratory data analysis (EDA) uses January-September 2018 only: 273 days and 78,624 timestamps. n1 ranges from 25.67\\r\\nto 29.36 m. The generator opens PUMP_1 below T1=2.4 m and closes it above 3.9 m [1]. Figure 2 shows how pressure, tank level\\r\\nand pump operation vary through the day; the mean curve does not describe every individual day.\\r\\nFigure 2. Own EDA: T1/PUMP_1 at left; n1/n4/n31 at right. Thin lines: individual days; red: clock-time mean.\\r\\nFor 78,621 valid +15 pairs, excluding the final three origins, persistence predicts the current n1 value. Its mean absolute error\\r\\n(MAE) is 0.0518 m (about 5.18 cm); root mean squared error (RMSE) is 0.0685 m and gives more weight to large errors. These are\\r\\nour 2018 descriptive results, not final test scores. Correlation with future n1 is 0.9923 for current n1, 0.9230 for T1, -0.0367 for\\r\\nPUMP_1 and -0.1574 for AMR_n1. Pressure changes slowly over 15 minutes; high correlation alone does not show that an extra\\r\\nsignal improves a forecast.\\r\\nFigure 3. Own EDA: six origin inputs and the future label. The label is separated for analysis and never enters X.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 3 \\r\\n Mini-Project Milestone 2\\r\\nLearning Models for Pressure Forecasting\\r\\nDS675\\r\\nAndres Arango Martinez\\r\\n3/5\\r\\n2. Contributions of Previous Authors\\r\\nWe searched BattLeDIM/L-Town predictive models and followed citations/code. Related papers/repositories form 35 groups of\\r\\nrelated works on L-Town or explicit modifications; external networks, reviews and tools were excluded. The five categories mean\\r\\nleak detection/location, current-pressure estimation, future-pressure/head forecasting, anomaly/sensor-fault detection, and sensor\\r\\nplacement. This inventory is not exhaustive.\\r\\nFigure 4. Own screening: percentages use 35 groups; gold identifies six selected subsets. Primary diagnostic purpose can include an intermediate\\r\\nfuture-pressure prediction.\\r\\nChebNet/SAM-kNN/AGN include future prediction; LILA/GATRes estimate current pressure; EILF explains Area C’s water\\r\\nbalance. SAM-kNN, AGN and GATRes use newly generated L-Town data rather than the released SCADA.\\r\\nStudy / task / code\\r\\nData preparation and how the method works\\r\\nReported results and what they show\\r\\nChebNet [3] +5-min\\r\\npressure and leakage\\r\\ndiagnosis. Code: [4].\\r\\nData: Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and\\r\\nthree past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split.\\r\\nModels: Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts\\r\\nhealthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current\\r\\npressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over\\r\\nseven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were\\r\\ncompared. Code fits scaling before the split.\\r\\n2019 diagnosis, Table 1: 19 accepted matches,\\r\\n138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61%,\\r\\nEUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300\\r\\nm while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain;\\r\\nthese results do not measure forecast-pressure error.\\r\\nSAM-kNN [5] +5-min\\r\\nvirtual pressure;\\r\\nanomalies. Code: [6];\\r\\nscenarios not shared.\\r\\nData: Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29\\r\\nsensors). Code averages three past readings from other\\r\\nsensors to predict the target’s next reading (+5 min);\\r\\npaper describes averaging four. Model: Adaptive\\r\\nnearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples\\r\\nand selects the memory with the lowest recent prediction\\r\\nerror.\\r\\nFive leak cases, Table 1: Each leak is detected,\\r\\nwith 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus\\r\\n11,146-19,216 for ordinary online kNN. These are\\r\\nalarm counts, not pressure errors. The scenarios are\\r\\nnewly simulated, not the released 2018-2019 series.\\r\\nAGN [7] Multistep\\r\\nfuture nodal heads.\\r\\nCode: no repository\\r\\nlinked; data on request.\\r\\nData: One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min\\r\\nsamples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10\\r\\ntrain/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use\\r\\nanalysis guides features. Model: Graph links share\\r\\ninformation; recurrent units retain history; attention\\r\\nweights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head +\\r\\nelevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs.\\r\\nFuture heads, Table 1: 12/24/48/96-step outputs\\r\\nreach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window,\\r\\nregional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium),\\r\\n32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole\\r\\nwindows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate\\r\\nn1(+15) score is reported.\\r\\nLILA [8] Current\\r\\nhealthy-pressure\\r\\nreference; leaks. Code:\\r\\n[9].\\r\\nData: Released SCADA; analyze pressure/flow\\r\\nrelationships to select reference sensors and healthy\\r\\nperiods. Model: Linear regression estimates current\\r\\nhealthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds\\r\\npersistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic\\r\\ncalculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw\\r\\nPUMP_1. It is not a future forecast.\\r\\nJournal Table 1: 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited\\r\\nleaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision\\r\\n89.5%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations\\r\\noutside 300 m count as false positives, not missed\\r\\nevents; recall=1 therefore does not mean perfect\\r\\nlocalization.\\r\\nGATRes [10] Current\\r\\nsparse-to-full pressure.\\r\\nCode: [11].\\r\\nData: 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20\\r\\ntrain/validation/test split); compare pressure\\r\\ndistributions with time-series test data. Model: Hide\\r\\nselected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph\\r\\nattention weights neighboring information to reconstruct\\r\\nthe hidden pressures at the same time point.\\r\\nOne-week test, Table 10: Across ten training\\r\\nruns, MAE=0.9501±0.0086 m versus 1.5928±0.0050\\r\\nm for the mGCN graph baseline. The ± is the\\r\\nstandard deviation between runs. This measures\\r\\ncurrent-pressure reconstruction, not +15-min\\r\\nforecasting.\\r\\nEILF [12] Engineering\\r\\ndiagnosis, not future\\r\\nlearning. Code: on\\r\\nrequest.\\r\\nData: 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump\\r\\ninflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector\\r\\noutflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min\\r\\nwindows to reduce level-rounding effects. Method:\\r\\nSeparate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL),\\r\\ndetect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically.\\r\\nJournal conclusion, p. 11: TPR=47.83%, five\\r\\nfalse positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak\\r\\ndiagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The\\r\\ntank-balance calculation is an existing engineering\\r\\ncontribution relevant to our feature selection.\\r\\nTP/FP/FN count accepted matches, false alarms/wrong matches and missed leaks; TPR=recall; F1 combines precision/recall.\\r\\nEUR is the competition monetary score [2]. Tasks/data differ, so these scores cannot rank n1 forecast accuracy. Graph filters,\\r\\nadaptive memories and CUSUM extend class baselines; their source files show the mechanisms [4,6,9]. Training was not reproduced.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 4 \\r\\n Mini-Project Milestone 2\\r\\nLearning Models for Pressure Forecasting\\r\\nDS675\\r\\nAndres Arango Martinez\\r\\n4/5\\r\\n3. Your Anticipated Contributions\\r\\n3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work\\r\\nWe propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level\\r\\nalternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [13,14];\\r\\nArtesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [15], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this\\r\\nexact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including\\r\\noperating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of\\r\\nworldwide priority.\\r\\nPlanned output\\r\\nWhat we expect to learn\\r\\nC1. Forecast-ready table\\r\\nWhich past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits.\\r\\nC2. Boosted-tree forecast\\r\\nWhether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest.\\r\\nC3. PAGE 4 
Mini-Project Milestone 2
Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
4/5
3. Your Anticipated Contributions
3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work
We propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level
alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [13,14];
Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [15], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this
exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including
operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of
worldwide priority.
Planned output
What we expect to learn
C1. Forecast-ready table
Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits.
C2. Boosted-tree forecast
Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest.
C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment
Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history.
3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope
Use 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven
weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check
units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit
Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that
all six signals help.
bpn1(u + 15) = FM(X(u)),
Fm(X) = Fm−1(X) + ηhm(X).
Boosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each
correction [18]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7.
Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [16]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows
[17]. They provide a linear and a tree-ensemble reference for the same task.
Initial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages {100,200,300} × learning rates {0.03,0.1}, depth=2,
minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max_features=1.0, depth
{6,10,14} × minimum leaf {10,40}. Ridge alpha: {0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set,
chosen by validation MAE [16,17,18]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.
3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement
Train January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the
2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that
cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based
assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1,
PUMP_1, n4/n31 or AMR_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.
Recompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE_model/MAE_persistence
(positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary
value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these
comparisons do not prove statistical significance. Deliver the preparation/model notebooks, settings, score table and prediction/error
plots. No learning model has been fitted. Delays/ages, balance proxies and block intervals remain outside this scope.

    "text": "Internal Error ()\nciteturn104view0 [wordlim: 200] Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 1\nL0: URL https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-15934-3_62 is not accessible via this tool.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nInternal Error ()\nciteturn104view1 [wordlim: 200] Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://doi.org/10.3390/w18172220\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 1\nL0: URL https://doi.org/10.3390/w18172220 is not accessible via this tool.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nLeakage Detection and Isolation in Water Distribution Network Based on Data Mining and Genetic Optimized Hydraulic Simulation | Zenodo (https://zenodo.org/records/3911523)\nciteturn104view2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: yesterday; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://zenodo.org/records/3911523\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 168\nL0: cite0†Image L1: \nL2: cite1†Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods 2020 L3: \nL4: Published June 28, 2020 | Version 1.0\nL5: \nL6: cite2†Report Open\nL7: \nL8: # Leakage Detection and Isolation in Water Distribution Network Based on Data Mining and Genetic Optimized Hydraulic Simulation\nL9: \nL10: ### Authors/Creators\nL11: \nL12:   * cite3†LIU,Runze^{1} L13:   * cite4†ZHANG,Zhen^{1} L14:   * cite5†ZHANG,Dian^{1} L15: \nL16: [Button: Show affiliations ]\nL17: \nL18:   * 1. DHI China\nL19: ## Description\nL20: Water loss due to leakages in water distribution network has been a major issue, it costs not only water resource and money but also brings potential water quality problems to the system. Researchers and engineers have been working on techniques detecting and isolating leakages in WDN and many approaches have been proposed and applied. Nowadays, the digitalization of urban infrastructures applies the SCADA in water distribution systems, which brings numerous data for analysis.\nL21: This research proposed a leakage detection and isolation approach for WDN, which could effectively accomplish the goal. The hydraulic model is first calibrated by applying genetic algorithm and WNTR simulation. The demand pattern and monthly seasonality for each user category are calibrated and defined by analyzing AMR data. Uncertain WDN parameters calibration are then done by genetic optimization.\nL22: Two data classification models (LSTM and LightGBM) are then used to learn how SCADA data and simulated data (especially pressure data) changes when leakages exist in network. The trained models show high accuracy on 2018 dataset and could predict suspicious leakage areas and time periods of certain leakages. Given this predicted information, a genetic-algorithm-based isolation method is applied to find out the exact leaking node or pipe.\nL23: Iterative hydraulic leakage simulations are run by EPANET engine and the results of each simulation are compared with SCADA data. The most matched simulation indicates the simulated leakage event is of high confidence being an actual leakage in network. Such detection and isolation approach is applied in L-Town, using 2018 SCADA data and reported leakage list. All 10 reported leakages in 2018 are detected by data classification models and isolated by the isolation algorithm.\nL24: The predicted leakage locations are close to the reported ones, indicating good performances of the proposed approach. The approach is then applied to detect and isolate leakages for L-Town in 2019.\nL25: ## Files\nL26: \nL27: ###\nL28: \nL29: Leakage Detection and Isolation in Water Distribution Network Based on Data Mining and Genetic Optimized Hydraulic Simulation_DHI China_LIU_ZHANG_ZHANG.pdf\nL30: \nL31: cite6†Preview L32: ###\nL33: \nL34: Files (926.1 kB)\nL35: \nL36: Name  | Size  |\nL37: --- | --- | ---\nL38: cite7†Leakage Detection and Isolation in Water Distribution Network Based on Data Mining and Genetic Optimized Hydraulic Simulation_DHI China_LIU_ZHANG_ZHANG.pdf md5:7ff14b2e8b1b7f0ad6c0ae255749cddb  | 926.1 kB  | cite6†Preview cite7†Download L39: \nL40: 638\nL41: \nL42: Views\nL43: \nL44: 487\nL45: \nL46: Downloads\nL47: \nL48: Show more details\nL49: \nL50:  | All versions  | This version\nL51: --- | --- | ---\nL52: Views  | 638  | 638\nL53: Downloads  | 487  | 487\nL54: Data volume  | 515.8 MB  | 515.8 MB\nL55: cite8†More info on how stats are collected.... L56: ## University of Exeter\nL17: ## Description\nL18: To learn from experience and to unleash the creativity, a novelty approach has been proposed with the main aim of detecting the different leakages of a fictitious L-town, containing a total of thirty three different pressure sensors in the whole infrastructure.\nL19: To tackle this problem, a new solution has been presented based on the prediction of the mean night pressure for each pressure sensor located in the infrastructure. After the predictions have been generated, the solution compares them with the real values to highlight those cases when the pressure was meaningfully lower than expected, being able to detect leakages in the infrastructure in a reliable way.\nL20: The machine learning algorithm has proven to be very accurate with barely 0.0235% of relative error rate, making a very reliable base for performing leakage detection. Based on those predictions a total of 109 different leakages have been found distributed inside 31 different pressure sensors.\nL21: As a main conclusion we can infer that it is possible to build a leakage detection system based on the anomalies found in the pressure during nights, with the help of the stability of this attribute and the great accuracy of the machine learning algorithm the solution was able to make predictions with barely 0.0235% of relative error rate and being able to identify 102 different leakages\nL22: ## \vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work}\par\vspace{.7mm}
We propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [\hyperlink{ref:13}{13},\hyperlink{ref:14}{14}]; Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [\hyperlink{ref:15}{15}], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of worldwide priority.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
{\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\hsize=0.57540\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.42460\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\rowcolor{Blue!13} \textbf{Planned output} & \textbf{What we expect to learn} \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C1. Forecast-ready table} & Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C2. Boosted-tree forecast} & Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment} & Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history. \\
\end{tabularx}\par}
\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Use 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that all six signals help.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\[\widehat p_{n1}(u+15)=F_M(X(u)),\qquad F_m(X)=F_{m-1}(X)+\eta h_m(X).\]
Boosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each correction [\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7. Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows [\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. Boosted trees can capture nonlinear relationships among pressure, tank storage, pump operation and local consumption; an advantage over these baselines must be tested.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Initial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages \{100,200,300\} Ã— learning rates \{0.03,0.1\}, depth=2, minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max\_features=1.0, depth \{6,10,14\} Ã— minimum leaf \{10,40\}. Ridge alpha: \{0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000\}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set, chosen by validation MAE [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16},\hyperlink{ref:17}{17},\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Train January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the 2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1, PUMP\_1, n4/n31 or AMR\_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Recompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE\_model/MAE\_persistence (positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Limited or absent auxiliary-signal value is also informative: it supports a simpler model. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these comparisons do not prove statistical significance. Deliver the preparation/model notebooks, settings, score table and prediction/error plots. No learning model has been fitted. Delays/ages, balance proxies and block intervals remain outside this scope.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
